However, research on this topic, particularly involving the production crew viewpoint, remains scarce within the construction production management domain. This study explores the effects of decentralisation (and in contrast, effects of centralisation) for construction production planning and control (PP&amp;C) from the combined perspectives of production crews and managers, and utilises these viewpoints to suggest improvements for PP&amp;C practices. The study answers the following research questions: How do decentralisation/centralisation affect construction PP&amp;C practices when considering both the production crew and manager perspectives? and Based on the aforementioned perspectives, how may construction PP&amp;C practices overall be improved? To achieve holistic assessment, the research is conducted as a multi-method comparative case study using survey-based social network analysis (SNA) and semi-structured interviews. The results show that decentralised PP&amp;C offers several benefits\u2014such as improved transparency, conflict resolution, commitment, and lower stress\u2014while allowing a proactive building of resilience, trust, ownership and autonomy for crews. In its current applied form, however, the approach does not fully reach the worker level. Regardless of the approach that is used, production crews perceive PP&amp;C as decentralised, while managers perceive PP&amp;C as having centralised structures. This gap between perceptions forms barriers for effective PP&amp;C that must be properly addressed. Eight improvement suggestions are constructed to improve PP&amp;C, that generally emphasise more deliberate decentralisation but that also indicate the necessity of partial central planning and control.",
    "description": "Decentralised, autonomous planning and control is a potential avenue of improvement in several fields, including construction. However, research on this topic, particularly involving the production crew viewpoint, remains scarce within the construction production management domain. This study explores the effects of decentralisation (and in contrast, effects of centralisation) for construction production planning and control (PP&amp;C) from the combined perspectives of production crews and managers, and utilises these viewpoints to suggest improvements for PP&amp;C practices. The study answers the following research questions: How do decentralisation/centralisation affect construction PP&amp;C practices when considering both the production crew and manager perspectives? and Based on the aforementioned perspectives, how may construction PP&amp;C practices overall be improved? To achieve holistic assessment, the research is conducted as a multi-method comparative case study using survey-based social network analysis (SNA) and semi-structured interviews. The results show that decentralised PP&amp;C offers several benefits\u2014such as improved transparency, conflict resolution, commitment, and lower stress\u2014while allowing a proactive building of resilience, trust, ownership and autonomy for crews. In its current applied form, however, the approach does not fully reach the worker level. Regardless of the approach that is used, production crews perceive PP&amp;C as decentralised, while managers perceive PP&amp;C as having centralised structures. This gap between perceptions forms barriers for effective PP&amp;C that must be properly addressed. Eight improvement suggestions are constructed to improve PP&amp;C, that generally emphasise more deliberate decentralisation but that also indicate the necessity of partial central planning and control.",

        <a href="/doi/permissions/10.1080/01446193.2022.2039399?scroll=top" id="permissionsLabel" class="show-permissions" aria-controls="show-permissions" aria-labelledby="reprints-perm" doi="10.1080/01446193.2022.2039399"><i class="fa fa-print" aria-hidden="true"></i>

        <div class="tab-content">
            <a id="top-content-scroll"></a>
            <div class="tab tab-pane active" role="tabpanel" id="mainTabPanel" aria-labelledby="showFullText">
                <article class="article">
                    
                    
                        
                        
                        
                            
                            <div class="hlFld-Fulltext"><div id="abstractId1" class="hlFld-Abstract"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="abstract">Abstract</h2><p class="last">Decentralised, autonomous planning and control is a potential avenue of improvement in several fields, including construction. However, research on this topic, particularly involving the production crew viewpoint, remains scarce within the construction production management domain. This study explores the effects of decentralisation (and in contrast, effects of centralisation) for construction production planning and control (PP&amp;C) from the combined perspectives of production crews and managers, and utilises these viewpoints to suggest improvements for PP&amp;C practices. The study answers the following research questions: <i>How do decentralisation/centralisation affect construction PP&amp;C practices when considering both the production crew and manager perspectives?</i> and <i>Based on the aforementioned perspectives, how may construction PP&amp;C practices overall be improved?</i> To achieve holistic assessment, the research is conducted as a multi-method comparative case study using survey-based social network analysis (SNA) and semi-structured interviews. The results show that decentralised PP&amp;C offers several benefits—such as improved transparency, conflict resolution, commitment, and lower stress—while allowing a proactive building of resilience, trust, ownership and autonomy for crews. In its current applied form, however, the approach does not fully reach the worker level. Regardless of the approach that is used, production crews perceive PP&amp;C as decentralised, while managers perceive PP&amp;C as having centralised structures. This gap between perceptions forms barriers for effective PP&amp;C that must be properly addressed. Eight improvement suggestions are constructed to improve PP&amp;C, that generally emphasise more deliberate decentralisation but that also indicate the necessity of partial central planning and control.</p></div><div class="abstractKeywords"><div class="hlFld-KeywordText"><div><p id="kwd-title-d7893015e126" class="kwd-title">Keywords: </p><ul aria-labelledby="kwd-title-d7893015e126"><li><a href="/keyword/Construction+production+management" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-gzj9="true">Construction production management</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/decentralisation" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-u79d="true">decentralisation</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/production+planning+and+control" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-yzy2="true">production planning and control</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/case+study" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-kh7e="true">case study</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/social+network+analysis" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-aeuf="true">social network analysis</a></li></ul></div></div></div><div class="collection-nav"><div id="related-articles-collection">This article is part of the following collection(s):</div><ul class="related-list" aria-labelledby="related-articles-collection"><li><a class="collection-rel" href="/journals/rcme20/collections/emerging-challenges">Confronting emerging challenges with interesting and important research in Construction Management and Economics</a></li></ul></div><div id="embedded-pdf-target" class="with-border"></div><div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="contentNavigationDropZoneAbs"><div class="widget gql-content-navigation none  widget-none" id="2ffb43c7-15dc-4c79-9e29-8e4bed9b3d1e">
        
        

        <div class="wrapped ">
            
            <div class="widget-body body body-none "><div class="ajaxWidget" data-ajax-widget="gql-content-navigation" data-ajax-widget-id="2ffb43c7-15dc-4c79-9e29-8e4bed9b3d1e" data-ajax-observe="true">
    
</div></div>
        </div>
    </div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0001"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e153">Introduction</h2><p>Construction projects form complex, networked and dynamic entities (Baccarini <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0001" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>, Bertelsen <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>) where success depends on effective production planning and control (henceforth PP&amp;C) (Koskela <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0043" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2000</a></span>). PP&amp;C is an essential part of production management that forms a process of deciding what and when to produce (and with which capacity and resources) and ensuring that the production is executed and steered accordingly (Vollmann <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0094" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1997</a></span>). Traditionally, construction PP&amp;C has relied on hierarchical governance, orchestrated by a master planner, but scholars have long questioned these centrally led practices for their effectiveness in managing increasingly complex production systems (Johnston and Brennan <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>, Koskela <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0044" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>). While centralised PP&amp;C does have its benefits, construction production could benefit from adopting more decentralised<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref fn"><a data-rid="EN0001" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>1</sup></a></span> management practices (Bertelsen and Koskela <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0009" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>).</p><p>Decentralised, autonomous decision-making has become a key element in effective project, organisation and production management (Laloux <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0048" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>). The benefits, such as increased productivity, improved worker well-being and enhanced agility to respond to changes, have been demonstrated in several fields, including information technology (Salovaara and Bathurst <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0080" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>), the military (Bertelsen and Koskela <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0009" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>, Marquet <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0059" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>, McChrystal <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0061" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>) and automobile manufacturing (Liker <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0053" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>). In construction, the decentralisation of PP&amp;C has previously been studied and implemented especially in the domain of lean construction through methods such as the Last Planner<sup>®</sup> System, or LPS (Ballard <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0004" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2000</a></span>), which has shown promising results in increasing production performance (Castillo <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0012" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>). Studies of construction industry culture indicate that employing decentralisation more widely would be a welcome change amongst industry professionals. Teräväinen <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0092" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>) state that people working in construction would prefer to operate in organisations and projects that embrace creativity, autonomy and a rule-breaking attitude rather than adhering to hierarchical structures.</p><p>Despite these promising ideas on how to improve construction management through more decentralised PP&amp;C, the viewpoints of production crews (which primarily consist of trade crew leaders and workers) are rarely investigated. Several authors, such as Loosemore (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0055" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>), Diekman <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0019" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2004</a></span>) and Hinze and Tracey (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0032" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1994</a></span>), have criticised the fact that even though production crews are vital to the construction supply chain and are essential parts of successful PP&amp;C, the presence of their viewpoints in construction production management studies is almost non-existent; instead, studies often focus on the managerial viewpoint of general contractors (GCs) or clients. Loosemore (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0055" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>) rightly argues that a far more balanced view within construction management research is needed.</p><p>Motivated by this research gap, this study aims to explore the effects of decentralisation (and in contrast, effects of centralisation) on construction PP&amp;C by taking into account the perspectives of both production crews and managers. Based on insights gained from these perspectives, suggestions are presented on how construction PP&amp;C practices could be improved overall. The aim is pursued through answering the following research questions:</p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">RQ1: How do decentralisation/centralisation affect construction PP&amp;C practices when considering both the production crew and manager perspectives?</p></li><li><p class="first last">RQ2: Based on the aforementioned perspectives, how may construction PP&amp;C practices overall be improved?</p></li></ul><p></p><p class="last">The remainder of this paper consists of five sections. First, the theoretical background section introduces literature related to the philosophical and theoretical underpinnings of centralised and decentralised management, current practices in construction PP&amp;C, and the perceived benefits, disadvantages and drivers of decentralised PP&amp;C on a general level. Next, to further investigate the production crew and manager perspectives of decentralised/centralised PP&amp;C, the methodology and results sections cover a comparative case study of two Finnish construction projects that utilised social network analysis (SNA) and semi-structured interviews. The discussion section reflects the results in light of the literature before the paper concludes with study contributions, limitations and future research suggestions. To maintain focus, the study is limited to planning and control of the production phase of construction projects, the units of analysis being construction crews, managerial teams and individuals in the production context.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0002"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e252">Theoretical background</h2><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0002-S2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e254">Centralised and decentralised management: philosophical and theoretical underpinnings</h3><p>Since the mid-twentieth century, mainstream engineering and construction management practices have been heavily influenced by a Platonian (i.e. introduced by the Greek philosopher Plato, 427–347 <span class="smallcaps smallerCapital">bce</span>) view of the world (Koskela <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0044" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>). In the Platonian view, knowledge can be deductively acquired from abstract ideas or universal truths and then put into action through a plan; events after the implementation of the plan and observations about the execution are of lesser interest. This view forms the centralised approach to management and has laid the foundation for the current dominant ways of PP&amp;C that Johnston and Brennan (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>) articulate as the theory of “management-as-planning” (MaP). Akin to the Taylorist approach, where management is primarily seen as autocratic action (Taylor <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0091" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1947</a></span>), MaP starts from an assumption that production consists of two independent parts: the plan is first centrally formed by an all-knowing manager and then precisely executed by production crews. However, scholars have increasingly criticised this approach over the past few decades; Johnston and Brennan (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>), for example, argue that this autocratic perception of management, in which management and action are separated, is a widely held but naive assumption that does not provide a realistic conception of the world or of production. Bertelsen (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>) and Pollack (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0073" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2007</a></span>) argue that forming an accurate plan that would separately guide actions effectively is impractical due to the complex, networked and dynamic nature of projects and production systems.</p><p class="last">An Aristotelian view of the world, introduced by Plato’s student Aristotle (384–322 <span class="smallcaps smallerCapital">bce</span>), provides an alternative approach. In the Aristotelian view, in addition to deduction, knowledge is acquired inductively, drawn from the observations and perception of those participating in the action (Koskela <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0044" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>). This view forms the decentralised approach to management, in which the viewpoints of actors and their responses to the present situation are essential. Johnston and Brennan (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>) articulate this view as “management-as-organizing” (MaO), in which the manager takes the role of coordinator and enabler, giving production crews and actors within crews authority in the PP&amp;C process. In this view, these actors are not seen as homogenous subordinates but instead as motivated individuals who are capable and willing to plan and control their own actions. In such cases, the system is iteratively improved by actors’ perceptions and insights. Koskela and Howell (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0045" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2002</a></span>) also argue that MaO is complementary with the language/action perspective, or LAP (Winograd <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0099" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1986</a></span>), in which organisations’ coordination processes are primarily formed by commitments and promises between actors instead of explicit plans or commands, forming two-way communication and decision-making paths rather than the one-way paths emphasised under MaP.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0002-S2002"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e302">Current practices in construction PP&amp;C</h3><p>Traditionally, researchers and practitioners have approached construction PP&amp;C from the Platonian viewpoint, viewing production as a linear, predictable entity that can be planned by a central authority and then precisely implemented by a project or site manager who makes decisions on behalf of production crews (Turner and Cochrane <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0093" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1993</a></span>, Morris <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0068" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1994</a></span>). As a result, the development of the most widespread PP&amp;C methods—such as the Critical Path Method (CPM; Kelley and Walker 1959), the Critical Chain (CC; Goldratt <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0026" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1997</a></span>) and the Line of Balance (LOB; Lumsden <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0056" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1968</a></span>)—have begun from the assumption of central management. Such approaches are explicitly designed to succeed in instances where production is perceived from the lenses of MaP (Johnston and Brennan <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>). Consequently, decision-making is often separate from the work itself, embracing a central viewpoint of management. Trade crews and individuals within them are often treated merely as executors of a predetermined plan rather than as independent decision-makers (Miller <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0065" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2002</a></span>, Watkins <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0096" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2009</a></span>, Priven and Sacks <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0074" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>), being disconnected from management decisions undertaken by the GC or client (Johansen and Wilson <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0038" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>, Laine <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0047" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2017</a></span>).</p><p>Some have suggested, however, that construction production could instead be treated from the Aristotelian viewpoint, taking into account the complex nature of construction projects (Winch and Kelsey <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0098" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>). Several studies indicate that during production, decisions mostly evolve through the interdependencies, expertise and self-organized actions of crews and individuals (Sawhney <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0083" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>, Watkins <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0096" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2009</a></span>, Ben-Alon <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0007" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>), which cannot be precisely planned or controlled by a single central entity (Winch and Kelsey <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0098" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>). As a result, scholars such as Ben-Alon <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0007" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>) and Sacks and Harel (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0079" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>) suggest that construction production naturally evolves to favour decentralised management that takes advantage of the perceptions of crews. Construction crews also seem to be motivated to conduct independent and autonomous work, as long as adequate managerial preconditions are provided (Coffey <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0015" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2000</a></span>, Schöttle <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0084" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2020</a></span>). These notions necessitate questioning the usefulness of the dominant centralised approaches and inviting new, decentralised management practices (Bertelsen <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>, Yammarino <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0100" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>) that will enable the autonomous decision-making of self-organized production crews and individuals within them (Janhonen <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0037" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>).</p><p class="last">Even though centralisation dominates current practice, scholars and practitioners have developed and successfully implemented various methods that emphasise decentralisation. Examples of these methods include the aforementioned LPS (Ballard <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0004" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2000</a></span>) and agile methods (e.g. Owen <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0070" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>), which tightly integrate crews and individuals into the decision-making process, while managers act more as facilitators than authorities (Howell and Ballard <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0034" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1998</a></span>). These methods tend to actively focus on the social side of the process in addition to focussing on the technical result, which has often been the sole focus (Bølviken <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0010" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>). It should be noted, however, that all the prevalent PP&amp;C methods can be implemented in a centralised or decentralised manner, even though decentralisation has not been the focus of their development. For example, combinations of CPM and LPS (Huber and Reiser <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0035" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>), the Location-Based Management System (LBMS) and LPS (Seppänen <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0088" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2010</a></span>), and takt production and LPS (Frandson <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0024" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>) have all demonstrated promising results in combining the technical and social viewpoints in the PP&amp;C process. In addition, methods such as LBMS and takt production can help to provide easily understandable visualisations of PP&amp;C processes and outcomes (e.g. Seppänen <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0088" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2010</a></span>), further aiding possibilities for collaboration and decentralisation.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0002-S2003"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e449">Benefits and disadvantages of decentralised PP&amp;C</h3><p>The benefits of decentralised planning and control have been widely demonstrated in various instances and across industries. Decentralisation has been connected to teams’ and individuals’ increased sense of ownership in operational decision-making (Mintzberg <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0067" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1976</a></span>), enhanced performance in project and production management, accelerated development of skills and continuous improvement, improved conflict management (Humphrey <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0036" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2007</a></span>, Yang and Guy <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0101" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2011</a></span>), enhanced short- and long-term proactivity and creativity (Grant and Ashford <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0028" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2008</a></span>), increased agility in responsiveness to changes and customer demands, better commitment to goals, and increased motivation and well-being of individuals (Mintzberg <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0066" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1983</a></span>, Richardson <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0078" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2002</a></span>). In construction, the implementation of decentralised PP&amp;C (through LPS, for example) has resulted in reduced process variability and waste with improved plan commitment, reliability (Priven and Sacks <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0074" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>), transparency, and project cost and time performance (Formoso and Moura <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0023" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2009</a></span>, Castillo <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0012" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>).</p><p class="last">While decentralisation is often seen as a mainly positive addition to planning and control practices that has several advantages, it also shows various disadvantages compared to centralised practices. Decentralisation may result in defects and inconsistencies in progress tracking (Barber <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0005" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1999</a></span>), inadequate risk management and excess risk-taking (Lanaj <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0049" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>), slow information flow and knowledge sharing (Mintzberg <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0066" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1983</a></span>), and coordination and communication problems amongst teams (Stinchcombe and Heimer <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0090" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1985</a></span>). These disadvantages are especially connected to situations in which adequate support and drivers for implementation are not provided (Salovaara and Bathurst <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0080" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>) and in the context of large-scale organisations in which the complexity involved in coordinating interdependent teams results in inefficiency (Drazin and Van de Ven <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0020" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1985</a></span>, Leavitt <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0051" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>). Salovaara and Bathurst (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0080" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>) note that, as with any new management style, the adoption of decentralisation often faces resistance while being both time-consuming and challenging to execute. In synthesis, centralised PP&amp;C may advocate for better big-picture information flow and consistency, overall risk management, and mutual coordination, especially in large and complex multi-team contexts and situations where support for decentralisation is inadequate.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0002-S2004"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e527">Drivers for effective decentralised PP&amp;C</h3><p>Several enabling drivers have been suggested in previous research within the general and construction management literature in order to succeed with decentralised PP&amp;C and to overcome its possible disadvantages.</p><p>First, decentralised management calls for competence in adaptive decision-making and resilience (Bertelsen and Koskela <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0009" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>, Pikas <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0071" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>, Saurin <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>, Janhonen <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0037" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>, Salovaara and Bathurst <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0080" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>, Martin <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0060" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>). The concept of resilience is often introduced in the context of managing complex systems (such as construction production) and denotes the ability to adjust and sustain systems’ performance before, during and after the occurrence of changing conditions or disturbances (Hollnagel <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0033" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>). Production crews and individuals need resilience to act in unexpected situations and to fill gaps between work-as-imagined (i.e. prescribed assumptions on how particular work is conducted) and work-as-done (i.e. how particular work is actually conducted). In complex systems, such gaps can be large (Soliman and Saurin <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0089" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2020</a></span>). Particularly in decentralised systems where crews and individuals have increased decision-making responsibility, increasing resilience by, for example, providing crews with scenario-based training and constant feedback in a real-life context is vital to maintain performance (Saurin <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>).</p><p>Second, scholars have suggested the building of trust and transparency of information within and between crews and individuals within them as drivers of decentralisation (Coffey <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0015" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2000</a></span>, Baiden <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0002" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>, Chinowsky <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0013" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2008</a></span>, Saurin <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>, Kärkkäinen <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0041" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>, Martin <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0060" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>). Together, trust and transparency allow individuals to express themselves freely without being blamed or punished (Howell and Ballard <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0034" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1998</a></span>) and increase the capacity for informed decision-making (Manu <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0058" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>). Loosemore (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0055" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>) states that by increasing trust and transparency, crews are more committed and can perform more effectively without the GC having to micro-manage their work.</p><p class="last">Third, study findings have suggested that crews should be empowered to act autonomously in decision-making (Zábojník <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0103" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2002</a></span>, Bertelsen <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>, Saurin <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>, Magpili and Pazos <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0057" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>, Pryke <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0076" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>). Increased autonomy correlates with a high level of plan reliability and commitment (Sacks and Harel <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0079" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>), increased worker motivation (Zábojník <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0103" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2002</a></span>), an improved ability to succeed in unexpected situations (Desai and Abdelhamid <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0018" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>), increased diversity of the perspectives that drive innovation (Saurin <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>) and the production system’s internal capacity in general (Bertelsen and Koskela <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0009" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2005</a></span>). To support autonomy, Bertelsen (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>) and Raelin (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0077" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>) argue that the project/site manager’s role should be transformed from a commander to a coach or facilitator who assists crews and individuals in providing the preconditions to perform their work.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0002-S2005"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e671">Synthesis</h3><p><button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="F0001" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Figure 1</button> presents a synthesis of the study’s theoretical background. Even though centralised PP&amp;C dominates construction management practices, implementing decentralised practices could be better suited for the needs of construction production. Indeed, methods that employ decentralisation have already been developed and successfully implemented. While decentralised PP&amp;C offers several benefits, adequate drivers are needed for effective implementation. The literature suggests that both centralised and decentralised approaches have certain benefits and disadvantages, indicating that some suitable combination could be the most effective way to conduct PP&amp;C.</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 1. </span></span> Synthesis of the theoretical background on centralised and decentralised planning and control.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="F0001" data-registered=""><img id="d1e683" src="/cms/asset/e7b45d0c-3745-4b89-8fda-7c2052e44b61/rcme_a_2039399_f0001_b.jpg" loading="lazy" height="477" width="500" alt="Figure 1. Synthesis of the theoretical background on centralised and decentralised planning and control."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="F0001" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Display full size</button></div></div></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0003"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e686">Methodology</h2><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0003-S2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e688">Research design</h3><p>A comparative case study with a multi-method approach was employed to further evaluate how decentralisation/centralisation affects PP&amp;C practices. The use of comparative case studies allows to investigate RQs in their real-life context (Yin <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0102" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>), thereby enabling systematic comparisons of two diverse viewpoints and drawing suggestions for possible improvement actions (Seawright and Gerring <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0087" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2008</a></span>). In this study, a single case consists of a production context of one construction project, including several sub-cases of work tasks formed around construction crews.</p><p class="last">The case studies include two steps, in which both quantitative and qualitative research methods were used. First, a survey-based social network analysis (SNA) was conducted to objectively understand how communication and decision-making structures are formed during production, providing objective insights on the utilised PP&amp;C practices. Second, semi-structured interviews were conducted with workers, crew leaders and site/project management to elucidate the SNA results and to explore the personal views of the project personnel towards PP&amp;C practices. Schröpfer <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0085" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2017</a></span>) and Pryke (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0075" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>) argue that by utilising such multi-method approach, researchers can address the complex and unique qualitative elements of construction projects while capturing the networks’ quantitative aspects.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0003-S2002"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e709">Case selection and descriptions</h3><p>The case selection was subject to the following criteria. First, because a comparative evaluation of decentralised versus centralised PP&amp;C approaches needed to be allowed, the cases had to represent both the centralised and decentralised approaches. Seawright and Gerring (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0087" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2008</a></span>) describe this strategy as a selection of the most different cases, which enables researchers to make stark comparisons between two approaches. They argue that to enable successful comparisons, cases must be comparable under a given dimension (in this instance, the approach to PP&amp;C) while different enough to allow researchers to draw meaningful conclusions and make causal inferences. Kaarbo and Beasley (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0040" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1999</a></span>) note that the cases need not be comparable under every dimension, as long as they represent similar contexts (i.e. a construction production context). Second, the selected cases had to allow for the inspection of the production context as a whole but also specific crews and their work tasks; this approach allows researchers to understand how the production operates as a whole (Wellman and Berkowitz <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0097" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1988</a></span>) while enabling a focus on the specific PP&amp;C of tasks that are studied as embedded sub-cases. Third, information-rich cases were preferred (Creswell and Clark <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0016" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2017</a></span>) that provided easy access to project information, documentation, site visits and project meetings, as well as the possibility of conducting SNA and interviews. The selected cases and work tasks had to be in progress during the data collection period to enable this scenario.</p><p>After the initial mapping of potential cases (which involved contacting and interviewing contractor and client representatives), two cases were selected from five candidates as most adequately fulfilling the study’s criteria. The five candidates consisted of two clusters: two building and three infrastructure projects. The two building projects represented the decentralised approach, while the three infrastructure projects represented the centralised approach. A case from each category was then selected to enable comparative evaluation. Due to the availability of meaningful information and the need for access to settings where the whole production and several individual crews could be inspected, three of the cases were rejected, and a study set of two cases was ultimately established.</p><p>Case 1 is a commercial office renovation project in Espoo, Finland, that consists of the modernisation of a 25,000 m<sup>2</sup> space. Production is divided into four phases; at the time of data collection, the third phase was in the second half of completion. Case 1 project management and PP&amp;C practices are characterised as decentralised, in which integrated project delivery (IPD) and “Big Room” working enabled partnering and collaborative decision-making with the client, contractors and designers. LPS is also used in PP&amp;C processes. The studied crews, which primarily consisted of trade contractors’ crew leaders and workers, operated with the following work tasks: (i) electricity works, (ii) ventilation and air conditioning (VAC) works, (iii) lock installation and (iv) painting. All the tasks were in progress during the data collection period.</p><p class="last">Case 2 is a roadwork renovation project in Helsinki, Finland, spanning ∼1.1 km of a two-lane street. The project includes the renovation of roads, traffic and municipal systems and the installation of new tram lines. The project is projected to span 2 years; at the time of data collection, the first of six production phases was halfway complete. Case 2 project management and PP&amp;C practices are characterised as centralised, with design-bid-build (DBB) contracting and limited initiated collaboration in decision-making processes. The case has several clients, including the city and several owners of underground utilities, and their requirements are coordinated centrally by a construction management organisation and a GC. The specific PP&amp;C method used in the case is undefined, but the approach contains elements from CPM and LBMS, operated through a heavily centralised approach. The studied crews, which consisted of both GC and trade contractor crew leaders and workers, operated with the following tasks: (i) earthworks, (ii) pipeworks #1 and (iii) pipeworks #2 (pipeworks were divided into two task sets, operated with separate crews). These were the primary ongoing tasks during data collection.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0003-S2003"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e731">Data collection</h3><p><button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="t0001" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Table 1</button> presents the case summaries and data collected in the study. In total, 15 interviews (in which two were identification interviews, further explained below) were conducted and 53 survey responses were collected; 24 survey responses and 5 semi-structured interviews are related to Case 1, and 29 survey responses and 8 semi-structured interviews are related to Case 2. In addition, supporting observation data was collected through several site and site-meeting visits and through the inspection of site-meeting minutes, organisational charts and production schedules. Data collection took place between November 2018 and April 2019. The data was consecutively collected first from Case 1 and then from Case 2.</p><div class="tableView"><div class="tableCaption"><div class="short-legend"><h3><p class="captionText"><span class="captionLabel">Table 1. </span> Case descriptions and data sources.</p></h3></div></div><div class="tableDownloadOption" id="t0001-table-wrapper"><a class="downloadButton btn btn-sm" role="button" href="/action/downloadTable?id=t0001&amp;doi=10.1080%2F01446193.2022.2039399&amp;downloadType=CSV">Download CSV</a><a data-id="t0001" class="downloadButton btn btn-sm displaySizeTable" href="#" role="button" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Display Table</a></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_3" id="S0003-S2003-S3001"><h4 class="section-heading-4" id="d1e884">Social network analysis</h4><p>SNA was chosen as the first research method because it is suitable for analysing project communication and decision-making structures objectively and holistically in both numeric and visual terms (Chinowsky <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0013" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2008</a></span>, Lee <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0052" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>) while allowing the identification of relationship patterns and individuals’ roles within networks (Pryke <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0075" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>, Zhang and Ashuri <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0104" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>). The approach can therefore provide insights into the centralisation/decentralisation of PP&amp;C processes, knowledge about their effectiveness, and aids in determining how these processes can be most effectively managed and further improved (Priven and Sacks <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0074" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>, Poleacovschi <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0072" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2017</a></span>).</p><p><button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="t0002" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Table 2</button> shows the relevant SNA observations for this study. The observations are drawn from two different categories: network structure and individual actor (node) attributes (Mead <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0062" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2001</a></span>). For network structure, findings related to tie characteristics, “factions,” network density and power distribution were primarily considered. For actor attributes, findings about in- and out-degree centrality, closeness centrality, betweenness centrality and eigenvector centrality were considered. These actor-related observations were used to detect the individuals’ roles in the network and are divided into three categories: group members/information consumers, liaisons/information hubs and authorities/“stars” (Freeman <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0025" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1977</a></span>).</p><div class="tableView"><div class="tableCaption"><div class="short-legend"><h3><p class="captionText"><span class="captionLabel">Table 2. </span> Descriptions of relevant observations and their interpretation.</p></h3></div></div><div class="tableDownloadOption" id="t0002-table-wrapper"><a class="downloadButton btn btn-sm" role="button" href="/action/downloadTable?id=t0002&amp;doi=10.1080%2F01446193.2022.2039399&amp;downloadType=CSV">Download CSV</a><a data-id="t0002" class="downloadButton btn btn-sm displaySizeTable" href="#" role="button" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Display Table</a></div></div><p>In total, three different social network models were constructed for both cases. The first model considers general communication (information-flow networks: observations of tie characteristics, factions and network density), while the second considers decision-making (decision-making networks with observations of power structures). To gain a holistic view of the production, these models were constructed using a whole-network approach, where the unit of analysis is the whole production network (Wasserman and Faust <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0095" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1994</a></span>). The third network model considers crew and task-level communication (PP&amp;C networks), divided into crew-specific subcategories. To analyse the crews’ internal dynamics and individuals’ influence in PP&amp;C in more depth, this network model was constructed using an “egocentric” approach, where the unit of analysis is specific work tasks and their related crews (Wasserman and Faust <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0095" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1994</a></span>).</p><p>In all three models, nodes represent individual actors. In information-flow and PP&amp;C networks, the edges represent two-way communication between actors; in decision-making networks, the edges represent one-way commands from one actor to another in order to address power structures. Boundaries for the analysis were set to include all actors who had a relevant effect on production during data collection, including site personnel, designers, GC and client representatives, material suppliers, etc. The rough boundaries were set by the researchers (using the nominalist approach) and iterated with case actors using the realist approach, further explained below (Laumann <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0050" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1989</a></span>).</p><p>Guided by Chinowsky <i>et&nbsp;al</i>.’s (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0014" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2010</a></span>) approach, the data for SNA was collected in two steps, in which the initial identification of the relevant actors was followed by primary data collection through a survey. First, the initial identification of the relevant actors was achieved by analysing project documentation (such as organisational charts) and conducting a single structured identification interview with a project representative (e.g. project manager) who had a holistic view of the most relevant actors. This initial identification allowed to draft rough social networks that then formed a basis for identifying and engaging the relevant actors with the survey. The following questions were asked during the identification interviews:</p><p><i>Information-flow and decision-making networks:</i></p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">1a. Which project participants are relevant for the production at the moment?</p></li><li><p class="first last">1b. How do these project participants communicate with each other (intensity, forms of communication)?</p></li></ul><p></p><p><i>PP&amp;C networks:</i></p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">2a. Which project participants are relevant for the task at the moment?</p></li><li><p class="first last">2b. How do these project participants communicate with each other (intensity, forms of communication)?</p></li></ul><p></p><p>Second, the primary data was collected through a survey. Surveys are widely adopted instruments for data collection in SNA studies (Chinowsky <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0014" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2010</a></span>, Schröpfer <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0085" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2017</a></span>). A survey is a flexible way to collect data (compared to analysing databases or email logs, for example) and has a more standardised structure compared to qualitative methods such as interviews (Nardi <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0069" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>). In the survey, the participants were asked to identify their communication and decision-making patterns regarding the case and the work tasks under observation. The participants were given an option to complete the survey in either electronic or paper form due to access restrictions; some participants (e.g. particular designers working in distant locations) were unable to physically fill in the forms, while others (e.g. particular workers) lacked direct access to computers or smartphones. The participants could choose either the Finnish or English version of the survey. The researchers provided active guidance for participants as any questions about the survey arose. The survey was sent to all 68 actors who were identified in the two cases; in total, 53 completed surveys were obtained, for a 78% response rate.</p><p>During the data collection period, it was noted that not all the participants who had initially been identified could be considered relevant for the production context. After these participants had been contacted to fill out the survey, they stated that they were not actively involved in the current stage of production. These participants included sales personnel or personnel involved in the project only in previous phases, for example. Thus, it was justified to omit these participants from the survey, concluding that the responses obtained were adequate to form social networks for meaningful analysis.</p><p>The survey consisted of five sections: (i) general information, (ii) questions about general communication (the information-flow network), (iii) questions about power structures (the decision-making network), (iv) questions about specific work tasks related to the crews (PP&amp;C networks) and (v) open-ended questions about decision-making (to qualitatively broaden and support the interview data). The survey is presented in <a href="#APP0001" data-behaviour="scroll" data-behaviour-ref="#APP0001" data-behaviour-callback="focus" data-behaviour-callback-target="#APP0001" data-registered="">Appendix 1</a>. The questions were presented in free-recall and free-choice format to enable participants to identify the actors they communicated with without predetermined restrictions. This approach allowed the participants to have freedom in their responses and to identify new actors, thus diminishing the possible biases of the initial identification stage (Wasserman and Faust <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0095" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1994</a></span>). The survey takers were provided with choices to determine communication modes (email, project document bank, phone, face-to-face, meeting, other) and for communication intensity. A five-point Likert scale, which is often used in survey-based SNAs (Hatala <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0030" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>), was employed to examine communication intensity more widely than with a binary scale while providing more tangible choices for participants than with a sliding scale. The score intensity was multiplied for the analysis as follows:</p><ol class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-order"><li><p class="first last">Seldom (once or twice per month) = edge weight 1.0;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Irregularly (a few times a month) = edge weight 2.0;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Regularly (once or twice a week) = edge weight 4.0;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Often (several times a week) = edge weight 10.0;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Daily (at least once a day) = edge weight 20.0.</p></li></ol><p></p><p class="last">The survey form was similar for everyone, but in section iv, the attendees were guided to focus on those subsections related to the specific work tasks which they had directly or indirectly participated. The survey forms were collected, combined in Excel and then pseudonymized; Gephi software (version 0.9.2) was utilised to construct the data into sociograms and to calculate the desired metrics. A force-directed layout (the Force Atlas algorithm) was used to enhance visualisation in Gephi.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_3" id="S0003-S2003-S3002"><h4 class="section-heading-4" id="d1e1125">Interview process</h4><p>In total, 13 semi-structured interviews (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="t0001" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Table 1</button>) were conducted to understand how individuals perceived the PP&amp;C practices in use. Semi-structured interviews were employed to allow interviewees to express themselves freely while still having a structure to guide the discussion towards the RQs. Even though the interviews followed a pre-set structure, the interview process was affected by the flow of the conversation and the interviewees’ expressions.</p><p>Interviewees were selected to cover two categories. The first category included project personnel in management and vital decision-making roles, such as project and site managers. The second category consisted of crew members such as crew leaders and workers, selected to cover every studied crew and its related work task. The participants for the semi-structured interviews were selected based on the initial identification interviews (see the previous chapter). In the first category, personnel with numerous strong ties within the project/site organisation who also had managerial roles in the project were preferred in the selection; in the second category, it was ensured that at least one person from every studied crew was interviewed.</p><p class="last">The interviews were structured with five themes: (i) general questions and reflections on SNA results, (ii) general communication, (iii) PP&amp;C practices, (iv) possible improvement actions and (v) ending notes. In theme iii, different questions were weighted for interviewees based on their category. The interview form is presented in <a href="#APP0002" data-behaviour="scroll" data-behaviour-ref="#APP0002" data-behaviour-callback="focus" data-behaviour-callback-target="#APP0002" data-registered="">Appendix 2</a>. The interviews were recorded verbatim and transcribed, but the quotes presented in the findings section have been translated from Finnish and lightly edited for clarity in English.</p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0003-S2004"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1138">Data analysis and synthesis</h3><p class="last">Data analysis/synthesis was conducted in four steps. First, a preliminary SNA consisting of visualisations was conducted to support and guide the interview process. Second, the social networks and interviews were analysed separately on a case-by-case basis. Both social network and interview analyses were deductively guided by the RQs (Creswell and Clark <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0016" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2017</a></span>) while seeking points of similarity/difference or new information. The previously transcribed interview answers were first structured and labelled in a spreadsheet by cases, interviewees, interview themes and interview questions. The interviews were then interpreted by looking for recurring themes and sub-themes, similarities and contrasting opinions, and emerging relationships between answers (Miles and Huberman <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0064" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1994</a></span>). The main author conducted the first two steps, which formed a base for further analysis and discussion between authors. Third, SNA and interview results were combined within cases to gain a holistic understanding of the cases (Yin <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0102" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>). The SNA and interview findings were also triangulated with the supporting data (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="t0001" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Table 1</button>) to gain support for the most prevalent findings and to explore possible new viewpoints that were not evident from the primary data sources. This step was followed by comparative case analysis, in which similarities, differences and complementary points of the centralised/decentralised PP&amp;C approaches were sought, thus forming a basis to interpret the RQs. Finally, the results were discussed in light of the literature and RQs, leading to a formulation of conclusions, study contributions and suggestions for future research avenues.</p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0004"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e1154">Findings</h2><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0004-S2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1156">General communication and power structures</h3><p>The general communication patterns were analysed through information-flow networks, illustrated in <button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="F0002" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Figure 2</button>. Regardless of the PP&amp;C approach, strong ties and dense network structure within the site indicated trust and transparency of communication in both cases. Factions with strong ties were formed between groups with frequent access to face-to-face communication, primarily around work tasks and site managers.
