Designers, client representatives and material suppliers were mostly connected to the site organisations through site managers and crew leaders via weaker ties.</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 2. </span></span> Case 1 and Case 2 information-flow networks.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="F0002" data-registered=""><img id="d1e1169" src="/cms/asset/99dce41b-bbcf-4a55-9e9b-c55707cf87b8/rcme_a_2039399_f0002_b.jpg" loading="lazy" height="500" width="379" alt="Figure 2. Case 1 and Case 2 information-flow networks."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="F0002" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Display full size</button></div></div><p>In Case 1, which used the decentralised PP&amp;C approach, the GC’s site managers acted as primary information liaisons, distributing information between project participants and the site personnel. Trade contractor crew leaders formed secondary information hubs, acting as links between managers and workers while also communicating actively together; they showed high values for betweenness, closeness and eigenvector centralities (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="F0003" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Figure 3</button>). Communication factions were primarily formed around work-specific crews. No significant information bottlenecks were noted, although the project organisation seemed to have a certain hierarchy of managers, crew leaders and workers, despite the decentralised approach to management.</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 3. </span></span> Case 1 and Case 2 information-network betweenness, closeness and eigenvector centralities.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="F0003" data-registered=""><img id="d1e1183" src="/cms/asset/9eb13c71-18af-49e2-a8d5-b3636a8f6515/rcme_a_2039399_f0003_b.jpg" loading="lazy" height="249" width="500" alt="Figure 3. Case 1 and Case 2 information-network betweenness, closeness and eigenvector centralities."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="F0003" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Display full size</button></div></div><p>In Case 2, which used the centralised PP&amp;C approach, site managers and tight task-specific crews consisting of crew leaders and workers formed the primary communication factions (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="F0002" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Figure 2</button>). Compared to Case 1, the site managers’ role as information liaisons seemed to be significantly more comprehensive, with high betweenness and eigenvector centrality measurements (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="F0003" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Figure 3</button>). Beyond the managerial level, however, the information flow seemed to be relatively evenly distributed amongst crew leaders and workers, who seemingly had an equal role as information liaisons and consumers. This finding, quite surprisingly, contrasted with the situation of Case 1, where crew leaders had a seemingly dominant role in information distribution compared to workers, despite the decentralised practices in place.</p><p class="last">The power structures, analysed through decision-making networks, shared similar patterns as the general communication. Regardless of the PP&amp;C approach, site managers were identified as primary authorities (high in-degree and closeness centrality), having a dominant role in distributing information and having authority over general decision-making. In Case 1, the decision-making power was relatively evenly distributed amongst site managers and crew leaders. In Case 2, the power was centralised to site managers. Below managers, however, the workers seemed to have a certain amount of autonomy (shown in evenly distributed in-degree centrality) compared to Case 1.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0004-S2002"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1195">Managers’ personal perceptions of PP&amp;C</h3><p>The data from the manager interviews supported the findings from the communication and decision-making network analysis; regardless of the approach, all the interviewed managers reflected on having a critical role in information distribution and having a vast amount of decision-making responsibility. Client representatives and project managers felt they had control of the overall schedule (Interviewee [I]12/Case [C]2 and I13/C2). Site and trade contractor managers felt they had control of and responsibility for phase schedules and site progress (including resourcing, design management and logistics management) while taking input on PP&amp;C from designers, managers and client representatives in weekly/monthly meetings and from crew leaders and workers in daily conversations (I01/C1, I05/C1, I06/C2, I07/C2, I08/C2).</p><p>Between the cases, the most prevalent differences came from the perception of how managers felt about collaboration. In Case 1, even though they felt they had certain decision-making responsibilities, the interviewees also expressed the importance of the collaborative input of production crews in PP&amp;C (I01/C1, I05/C1).</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">I05/C1 [Project manager, VAC]: <i>We work things out together</i> [with site management and crew leaders]<i>.… The crew leaders have the best awareness of the production status, and we often do the</i> [three-week] <i>look-ahead and weekly planning together.</i></p></div><p>In Case 2, the long- and short-term plans were perceived to be primarily orchestrated by the project and site management (I07/C2, I08/C2, I12/C2). Even though managers reflected on taking suggestions from crew leaders and workers, they felt that the decisions were mainly made in a centralised manner.</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">I07/C2 [Responsible site manager]: <i>The workers always have their viewpoint</i> [on how to conduct work] <i>… of course, we listen to their opinions, but managers always have the responsibility in actual decision-making.</i></p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0004-S2003"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1219">PP&amp;C structures</h3><p>In both cases, compared to the information-flow and decision-making networks, the predominant roles shifted within the PP&amp;C networks. In Case 1, crew leaders took a prominent role in task-level PP&amp;C (with high values for closeness, betweenness and eigenvector centralities), followed by workers, project managers and site managers. Particularly regarding the PP&amp;C of the electricity, locking and painting tasks, crew leaders dominated as liaisons/stars. Within crews, the PP&amp;C activities were managed centrally through crew leaders, as they dominated information flow between crew members, site management and other site crews. This finding indicated certain limitations in information transparency that formed a possible bottleneck for effective communication.</p><p>A similar shift of liaison and star roles was partially present in Case 2, but from site managers equally to the whole task-specific crew. Compared to Case 1, the PP&amp;C information flowed more freely within the crew, indicating decentralisation with a large amount of trust and transparency. The crew members acted equally as information liaisons and consumers, thus supporting the information-flow network analysis findings. Site managers still partially remained central authorities, and they were primarily responsible for communication from the site to other project participants. <button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="F0004" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Figure 4</button> illustrates the PP&amp;C networks for electricity works (Case 1) and pipeworks 1 (Case 2).</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 4. </span></span> PP&amp;C networks of electricity works (Case 1) and pipeworks 1 (Case 2).</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="F0004" data-registered=""><img id="d1e1233" src="/cms/asset/77398ec4-2e61-4f1e-8b48-8deb61e3ea69/rcme_a_2039399_f0004_b.jpg" loading="lazy" height="500" width="398" alt="Figure 4. PP&amp;C networks of electricity works (Case 1) and pipeworks 1 (Case 2)."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="F0004" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-registered="">Display full size</button></div></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0004-S2004"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1236">Production crews members’ personal perceptions of PP&amp;C</h3><p>Regardless of whether a centralised or decentralised approach was used, the interview findings of the production crews supported the responsibility shift observed from whole networks to PP&amp;C networks. In contrast to the managers’ perception, in both cases, crew members felt they had a vast amount of responsibility and autonomy towards PP&amp;C coordination, especially on weekly and daily activities. In Case 1, crew leaders and workers generally felt that even though project and site managers were responsible for large-scale decisions, during production, most decisions about PP&amp;C (including coordination with other crews, task execution and resource management) were coordinated primarily by crew leaders and also partially by workers (I02/C1, I03/C1, I04/C1). The crew members felt that crew leaders had the best overall understanding of the production status, thus supporting the SNA findings. For the crews, Case 1’s site managers were not viewed as decision-makers but more as information hubs and facilitators, and they often provided requirements about plan progression that were collaboratively discussed and then refined by crews, led by crew leaders (I02/C1, I03/C1, I04/C1).</p><div class="quote"><p class="first">I02/C1 [Electricity worker, partially acting as crew leader]: <i>I make decisions about the work order for electrical work regarding the whole worksite.</i></p><p>I03/C1 [Painter, partially acting as crew leader]: <i>Site managers pass on a large amount of information, but they aren’t that involved in daily or weekly decisions. I make most of the decisions regarding planning and control on the site … I feel that I can contribute as much as I want. And I also try to involve other workers in the decision-making as much as I can.</i></p><p class="last">I04/C1 [Lock installer, partially acting as crew leader]: <i>Well, in fact, nobody does the planning for me. I’ll do it on the run and apply as needed.… I’ll make all the decisions about the workflow, and then guide other workers.</i></p></div><p>Many workers felt that they had adequate room for their own PP&amp;C decisions in Case 1 (open-ended survey questions).</p><div class="quote"><p class="first">[Painter 1/C1]: <i>I make decisions on the order of work every day, deciding on the work stages and the order of floors and rooms.</i></p><p class="last">[Electricity worker 1/C1]: <i>I make decisions on the final placement of the furnishings (every week), cable routes (every week) and fixing/updating of insufficient electricity plans (every month).</i></p></div><p>Similar results were found in Case 2, with the difference that the responsibility of weekly and daily decisions not only primarily lay with crew leaders but also equally with workers (I09/C2, I10/C2, I11/C2). While in Case 1 the collaborative PP&amp;C was primarily initiated through weekly LPS meetings that included managers and crew leaders, in Case 2, the collaboration was more deliberately extended to include all production crew members (also observed from the supporting documents). The workers also felt they had the best overall understanding of tasks’ weekly, daily and production progress. Even though site managers had a certain central authority and were responsible for managing the overall schedule and supporting tasks such as traffic coordination, workers felt that they played a significant role in adjusting and coordinating weekly and daily task-level work. The interviewees saw tight collaboration and adjustment between site managers and crews as necessary for success. They also emphasised the importance of adequate transparency in and between crews, which enabled the workers to control their daily work semi-autonomously (I09/C2, I10/C2).</p><div class="quote"><p class="first">I09/C2 [Excavator operator]: <i>I make daily decisions on the work order and general organization of work … I like the independence … site managers have a lot to coordinate, so it’s also reasonable to decentralize the authority.</i></p><p class="last">I11/C2 [Pipe installer]: <i>Daily and weekly decisions</i> [about work execution] <i>are made in tight collaboration with site managers and other workers … however, if everything’s clear, the management doesn’t have to intervene in my decisions.</i></p></div><p>Regardless of the case, the crew leaders and workers were not frustrated with having this seemingly large role in PP&amp;C; rather, they seemed to feel that having the responsibility was a sign of a smooth and collaborative production process. The crew leaders and workers also seemed to have a large amount of control over design and material management (I02/C1, I03/C1, I04/C1, I09/C2, I11/C2). The detailed design was done in parallel with production in both cases, while crew leaders and workers also largely contributed to the final designs. On the other hand, crew leaders and workers did not necessarily desire to be involved in every decision, such as in overall coordination; instead, they were primarily interested in contributing to areas that were directly connected to their work.</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">I02/C1 [Electricity worker, partially acting as crew leader]: <i>The designer doesn’t run the designs for the accuracy that’s needed on the site … in lots of cases, we make suggestions for the designer, who then approves them.</i></p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0004-S2005"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1281">Perceived drivers and advantages of decentralisation/centralisation</h3><p>The interviewees saw the various drivers for decentralisation as being beneficial for PP&amp;C in general, regardless of the approach, although the drivers emerged differently between the cases and also resulted in different advantages and variability in performance. In Case 1, the interviewees perceived the collaborative contracts/delivery method, the Big Room concept and decentralised PP&amp;C methods as providing transparency and the ability to sustain their performance when problems or disputes arose (indicating resilience), especially amongst managers in the phase-planning stage. They also viewed these methods as enabling tighter coupling of designers, client representatives and the site organisation (I01/C1, I05/C1). Interviewee I02/C1 also mentioned that earlier collaboration in previous projects with the GC and other trade contractors had increased initial trust. The implementation of the above-mentioned practices enabled process-oriented and agile conflict and change management, in addition to a commitment to mutual production goals (I03/C1). The interviewees mostly mentioned having a sound sense of ownership over the process (indicating autonomy in decision-making), although this only seemed to apply to managers and crew leaders. These above-mentioned advantages were seen to positively contribute to schedule performance (I4/C1, I5/C1), and during the data collection period, the project was on time despite the tight initial milestones.</p><p class="last">In contrast, in Case 2, most of the interviewees (I06/C2, I07/C2, I08/C2, I10/C2, I11/C2) argued that DBB as a delivery method was relatively rigid, and the information flow between the design and production phases, and between organisations, was somewhat inadequate (I12/C2). The Case 2 interviewees generally admitted that the above-mentioned issues indicated a large gap between work-as-imagined and work-as-done, which in addition to having demanding site conditions and an initially tight schedule created certain stress for site personnel. They did perceive transparency of information, trust and the ability to solve unforeseen problems (indicating resilience) within the tight site organisation to be excellent, however; these factors developed during the production when managers, crew leaders and workers had to continuously tackle emerging problems together (I08/C2, I09/C2, I11/C2). The interviewees perceived this scenario to have resulted in gradually increased performance in production control and a strong sense of autonomy within production crews, albeit simultaneously resulting in dependability on the performance of key site personnel (particularly site managers) in managing risks and communication outside the site. During the data collection period, the project was slightly behind the initial schedule, but the site personnel were generally positive about being able to meet the upcoming milestones on time (I7/C2, I8/C2, I10/C2, I11/C2).</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0004-S2006"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1285">Improvement suggestions for PP&amp;C practices</h3><p>Several interviewees from both categories and both cases felt that if crew leaders and workers were included in PP&amp;C decision-making more intensively and earlier, then several other aspects of the project (e.g. design management, scheduling, work structuring and resource management) would be improved (I02/C1, I03/C1, I05/C1, I07/C2, I08/C2, I11/C2, I12/C2).</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">I12/C2 [Project manager]: <i>Designs and plans should also be done to serve workers, not only authorities … to improve, the involvement of crew leaders and workers in</i> [the early] <i>design and planning stages is critical.</i></p></div><p>But even though the interviewees saw the decentralisation of practices as having potential, participants from both cases also argued that some aspects of PP&amp;C (such as large-scale planning decisions, coordination between projects and larger material orders) could be adequately handled with centralised management (I02/C1, I09/C2). In addition, the current industry culture did not seem to support a radical increase in decentralisation (I03/C1, I11/C2).</p><div class="quote"><p class="first">I03/C1 [Painter, partially acting as crew leader]: <i>I think it would be beneficial to involve the people who actually do the work</i> [in decision-making]<i>. But I don’t know if they’d actually listen to my opinions …</i></p><p class="last">I11/C2 [Pipe installer]: <i>Of course, it would be useful to involve workers to contribute more in planning … but the system is what it is. I don’t know how it could be changed.</i></p></div><p class="last">Other suggested improvements, most of which considered an increased proportion of decentralisation, included allocating more time for individual workers’ work planning, involving workers more deeply in decision-making procedures, allocating more time to training individuals to cope with decision-making responsibility, and officially determining and recognising individuals’ responsibilities in places where their roles were not currently visible (I04/C1, I06/C2, I11/C2). In addition, one interviewee believed contracts should better address the needs of networked processes; currently, the capacity needed for crew leaders’ and workers’ work planning was not recognised or rewarded adequately (I03/C1). In the same vein, crew leaders and workers felt that the schedulers and managers did not sufficiently recognise the quantity of decisions, coordination and corrective actions that were actually needed in the daily and weekly PP&amp;C currently performed by crew leaders and workers. This resulted in increased workload generally but also underutilisation of their skills in the formal PP&amp;C process, despite the managers’ recognition of the need for worker involvement at some level (I02/C1, I03/C1, I09/C2).</p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0005"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e1310">Discussion</h2><p>In this chapter, RQ1 <i>How do decentralisation/centralisation affect construction PP&amp;C practices when considering both the production crew and manager perspectives?</i> is first discussed in sections 5.1 and 5.2, followed by discussing RQ2 <i>Based on the aforementioned perspectives, how may construction PP&amp;C practices overall be improved?</i> in section 5.3.</p><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0005-S2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1320">The gap between managers’ and crews’ perceptions hampers PP&amp;C effectiveness</h3><p>The study results indicate that regardless of the chosen approach towards PP&amp;C—in other words, whenever management is approached from the viewpoint of autocratic planning (MaP; Johnston and Brennan <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>) or autonomy-driven organising (MaO)—both Platonian and Aristotelian paradigms were present. From the viewpoint of production crews, the observations from both cases supported the presence of an Aristotelian, inductive paradigm, as production seemed to emerge as dynamic and decentralised (Ben-Alon <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0007" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2014</a></span>). The crew members felt primarily responsible for operational decisions, quantitatively making a majority of the decisions autonomously in a decentralised manner (supported by the PP&amp;C network analyses, and also noted by Pikas <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0071" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>). The crew leaders and workers had autonomy in planning and controlling their work (also noted by Bertelsen <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>) and did not seem to be frustrated with this vast amount of responsibility but primarily appreciated the possibility to contribute. Weekly and daily (i.e. micro-level) PP&amp;C activities were largely based on face-to-face, two-way communication and individuals’ commitment in tight site factions, rather than explicit orders (i.e. the language/action perspective; see Winograd <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0099" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1986</a></span>). These findings furthermore support the presence of MaO, in which individuals are motivated and capable of affecting their work, possessing an inherent drive for autonomy (also noted by Coffey <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0015" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2000</a></span>, Schöttle <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0084" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2020</a></span>).</p><p>In contrast, from the managerial perspective and amongst formal and phase (i.e. macro-level) planning processes, centralisation and hierarchical structures were present in both cases (indicating the presence of the Platonian, deductive paradigm). Partially in Case 1 and especially starkly in Case 2, which was categorised as centralised, managers felt they had (and also objectively seemed to have) a vast amount of responsibility and authority over decision-making. Even though in Case 1 the decentralised practices partially enabled the extension of formal PP&amp;C decision-making to crew leaders, recognition of the workers’ involvement was primarily missing from the managerial viewpoint.</p><p>These strong differences in perceptions towards PP&amp;C between managers and crews—which occurred regardless of the employed approach– require increased attention. Even though both sides’ viewpoints are justified, this contradiction in epistemological understanding of production’s nature surfaces problems for further improving PP&amp;C practices. As Johansen and Wilson (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0038" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2006</a></span>) point out, overly differing perceptions towards the epistemology of production is one of the most critical barriers to building effective PP&amp;C. If managers do not acknowledge a crew’s role as a vital decision-making entity, then the PP&amp;C practices will more likely continue to be built upon overly centralised and sub-optimized practices. If managers perceive production solely from the Platonian viewpoint, they cannot be fully aware of recurring daily problems, thus increasing the gap between work-as-imagined and work-as-done (Soliman and Saurin <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0089" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2020</a></span>), which will then further require an increased amount of resilience from the crews to not feel extensively overwhelmed or burnt out by their micro-level decision-making responsibilities (Salovaara and Bathurst <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0080" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>).</p><p class="last">Despite having a certain feeling of autonomy, the crew leaders and workers simultaneously felt that the critical roles of crews in operational PP&amp;C were not recognised, resulting in a lack of time, resources and support in decision-making, which further led to ineffectiveness, reactivity and increased stress. In this kind of environment, the site crew will most likely be unable to utilise their expertise in macro-level decisions, which will also lead to the waste of unused talent. Although addressing this gap between the perceptions is crucial for improving PP&amp;C practices, its presence is not very surprising, as the dominant management practices (such as CPM) have been built and advanced primarily from the Platonian viewpoint and only take into account the managerial viewpoint (Johnston and Brennan <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>).</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0005-S2002"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1369">Decentralised PP&amp;C offers several advantages proactively, but does not take workers into account</h3><p>Differences between the approaches were also noted. The first notable difference is that the decentralised approach to PP&amp;C seems to yield several advantages over centralised PP&amp;C that are primarily in line with those presented in the theoretical background section, also resulting in improved schedule performance. In Case 1, despite the partial presence of hierarchies, the approach allowed decentralisation on the managerial level and between crews, in which managers also acted as facilitators. Simultaneously, crews made their own decisions, which were orchestrated by the crew leaders. As an outcome of implementing decentralised PP&amp;C, the SNA results show increased transparency in the whole supply chain (indicated by the high amount of strong ties and smooth information flow), while the interview results show increased capability for conflict management between site crews and project stakeholders (see also Humphrey <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0036" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2007</a></span>, Yang and Guy <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0101" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2011</a></span>), increased proactive commitment to mutual goals (Priven and Sacks <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0074" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2015</a></span>) and lower stress (Mintzberg <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0066" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1983</a></span>). In contrast, in Case 2, PP&amp;C was dominated by the managers making decisions, which positioned them as critical individuals for successful information and decision-making flow (within the site and towards off-site stakeholders), thus creating opaqueness to the production process. This setup made the process more vulnerable to distortions and dependent on individuals at the site, which created a certain reactiveness to PP&amp;C practices.</p><p>The results indicate that the presence of the suggested drivers (resilience, transparency/trust, autonomy) aid successful PP&amp;C regardless of the managerial approach taken. As a second notable difference, with the decentralised approach the drivers for success are built proactively on an organisational level, are more process-based, and emerge with more ease. With the centralised approach, drivers seem to emerge from the urgency to cope with problems the site crew encounter. Thus, even though drivers are eventually achieved with both approaches, the decentralised approach seems to be better suited for systematic and collaborative capacity development, as also noted by Yang and Guy (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0101" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2011</a></span>). A successful centralised approach seems to require strong and experienced individuals who can perform under stress and manage production with less overall support, thus increasing the system’s vulnerability. The proposed benefits of the centralised approach, such as good large-scale information flow and mutual coordination (Mintzberg <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0066" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1983</a></span>), were present in both cases, although with the centralised approach they seemed to depend on capable individuals in key managerial positions and their ability to build the drivers reactively during production.</p><p>The third notable difference between the approaches is how the production crews’ internal dynamics and PP&amp;C responsibilities within crews materialise. Even though the approaches primarily emerge on the overall production level as indicated by the theoretical background, the used approach had a contrary than intended effect for the crews’ internal dynamics. Despite employing the decentralised approach, in Case 1, crew leaders dominated the micro-level decisions, forming centralised PP&amp;C structures within crews; the responsibility was shifted from managers to the crew leader, but other crew members were still excluded from the process. A sense of ownership of PP&amp;C was present (Mintzberg <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0067" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1976</a></span>) but primarily applied only to the managerial and crew leader levels. Thus, while the decentralised approach demonstrate various benefits, it is not (at least in its applied form) able to capture or improve the necessary aspects of production crew members’ needs, enabling decentralisation only in a limited way. These effects were primarily observed from the PP&amp;C networks and crew interviews, and they indicate that the current PP&amp;C (and other collaborative practices, such as collaborative delivery models) do not necessarily affect crews’ internal dynamics and micro-level PP&amp;C, even though that could have made a welcome addition to improving PP&amp;C effectiveness. In contrast, with the centralised approach, it was surprising how evenly the decision-making responsibility was distributed within crews, despite the presumption that the approach was expected not to allow workers to have a large amount of freedom in their own decision-making (e.g. Johnston and Brennan <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1996</a></span>). Even though managers possessed a vast amount of autocracy, the crew members were able to gain almost equally distributed responsibility with crew leaders in PP&amp;C above the managerial level.</p><p class="last">The results indicate that even though aiming for decentralisation, methods such as LPS might smother the voice of the workers with their extensive focus on project managers and crew leaders, namely “last planners”, as also indicated by Pikas <i>et&nbsp;al.</i>’s (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0071" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2012</a></span>) results. This scenario might increase rather than decrease the gap between epistemological perspectives towards PP&amp;C practices, leading to an inability to solve the problems brought up in the previous chapter. Also suggested by the benchmark of LPS development, one possible development action of the method is to extend the involvement from crew leaders to the worker level (Ballard and Tommelein <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0003" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2016</a></span>), which would seem an adequate action for improving decentralised PP&amp;C.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0005-S2003"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1418">Improvement suggestions</h3><p>Regardless of the managerial approach taken, the improvement suggestions for better PP&amp;C generally involve more deliberate decentralisation, stemming with the vast amount of observed benefits in the literature and the study findings that decentralised PP&amp;C could bring.</p><p>To diminish the gap between managers’ and crews’ perceptions towards PP&amp;C and improve overall PP&amp;C capabilities, recognition of a crew’s tacit responsibilities and internal dynamics (as also noted by Chinowsky <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0013" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2008</a></span>) should be embedded in formal PP&amp;C processes. Brought up especially in the workers’ and crew leaders’ interviews and supported by studies in the literature (e.g. Saurin <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>), more intense involvement of crew leaders and workers in planning, officially determining their responsibilities in operational decision-making, and allocating more time and providing training for role-based planning should be considered as improvement initiatives. Particular attention could also be paid to training site managers to ensure that they can effectively perform as facilitators, which would require a partially renewed skill set (Bertelsen <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>).</p><p>The drivers presented in the theoretical background were seen to be beneficial for the production, regardless of the degree of intended decentralisation. Deliberately building resilience (Saurin <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>); increasing trust by providing support for team-building with face-to-face communication (Chinowsky <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0013" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2008</a></span>), particularly if the team has not worked together beforehand; ensuring adequate transparency through visible information flow (Kärkkäinen <i>et&nbsp;al</i>. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0041" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>); and providing ownership and a sense of autonomy for all crew members over operative decisions (Bertelsen <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2003</a></span>) all provide preconditions for success. In addition to improving the above-mentioned project-level capabilities, these development actions should also reach the level of cultural change (also see Teräväinen <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0092" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2018</a></span>).</p><p class="last">But although increasing the amount of decentralisation and an emphasis on the Aristotelian viewpoint of PP&amp;C seems to be favourable, Koskela <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0044" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2019</a></span>) note that instead of purely opting for an either fully centralised or decentralised approach, a fine balance between the two is usually necessary for effective management. In addition to driving the above-mentioned suggestions, centralisation might be necessary in aspects such as project-level coordination and risk management (suggested by the interviewees and Lanaj <i>et&nbsp;al.</i> <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0049" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2013</a></span>), at least in short-term implementation initiatives and given the prevalent cultural environment.</p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0006"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e1482">Conclusion, limitations and avenues for future research</h2><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0006-S2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1484">Conclusion and study contributions</h3><p>This study has explored the effects of decentralisation (and for contrast, effects of centralisation) for construction production planning and control (PP&amp;C) from the combined perspective of production crews and managers, in order to utilise these viewpoints to provide improvement suggestions for construction PP&amp;C practices. To achieve holistic assessment, the research was conducted as a multi-method comparative case study using survey-based social network analysis (SNA) and semi-structured interviews.</p><p>From a theoretical perspective, the study increases the field’s understanding of the differences and similarities of decentralised and centralised PP&amp;C while considering the diverse perspectives of both managers and production crews. To answer the first research question <i>How do decentralisation/centralisation affect construction PP&amp;C practices when considering both the production crew and manager perspectives?</i> production crews tend to perceive PP&amp;C as dynamic and decentralised, while managers’ perception is geared more towards the existence of hierarchical and centralised structures, regardless of the approach that is used. If not addressed, this gap between the perceptions will continue to form barriers for building effective PP&amp;C practices, including overly centralised, managerially led practices; large gaps between work-as-imagined and work-as-done; site crews feeling overwhelmed and stressed; a lack of time, resources and support in crews’ operational decision-making; and the waste of unused talent.</p><p>Several differences also exist: the decentralised approach offers several benefits—such as providing transparency in the supply chain, the capacity to resolve conflict, commitment to mutual goals and lower stress—while allowing a proactive building of necessary drivers such as resilience, increased transparency/trust, autonomy and feeling of ownership, all of which are necessary, regardless of the managerial approach taken. These drivers lead to efficient and process-oriented PP&amp;C. The effects of decentralisation, however, do not directly reach the worker level in the used practices, which indicates that the current PP&amp;C practices do not consider the production crews’ internal dynamics very effectively but are instead limited to the managerial and crew leader levels, hampering reaching the full potential of decentralisation.</p><p>For practitioners, the study provides suggestions about how construction production could be more effectively managed by taking into account the possibilities of both decentralised and centralised planning and control. Considering the second research question <i>Based on the aforementioned perspectives, how may construction PP&amp;C practices overall be improved?</i> the suggestions are summarised as follows:</p><p><b>Suggestions related to decentralisation include</b></p><ol class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-order"><li><p class="first last">Recognising a crew’s tacit responsibilities and internal dynamics and embedding them in formal PP&amp;C processes;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Ensuring the earlier and more intense involvement of crew leaders and workers in planning, officially determining their responsibilities in operational decision-making, and allocating more time and resources for their individual PP&amp;C;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Providing training for role-based PP&amp;C, both for crew leaders and workers but also for managers to act as facilitators;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Providing support for building resilience, increasing trust with team-building and face-to-face communication, and supporting adequate information transparency;</p></li><li><p class="first last">Ensuring ownership of decisions and a sense of autonomy for crews; and</p></li><li><p class="first last">Developing cultural change towards the recognition of the benefits of decentralisation.</p></li></ol><p></p><p><b>Suggestions related to centralisation include</b></p><p>7.Ensuring project-level alignment with central coordination;</p><p>8.Ensuring overall risk management with central risk assessment and coordination.</p><p>The improvement suggestions generally emphasise more deliberate decentralisation of management practices, although they also indicate the necessity of partial central planning and control. Overall, these improvements could proactively reduce the barriers created by the differing perceptions, simultaneously opening avenues for enhanced production planning and control practices that take into account the potential of decentralisation.</p><p class="last">From an empirical perspective, the study’s setting may be considered as novel, as production crews’ (and especially workers’) perceptions of PP&amp;C have been studied relatively little. By using a multi-method approach, it was also possible to observe the perceptions objectively and subjectively, allowing triangulation of the findings.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0006-S2002"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1519">Limitations</h3><p>A comparative case study consisting of social network analysis (SNA) and interviews was selected as a research method to explore phenomena in-depth and to allow triangulation (Eisenhardt and Graebner <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0021" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2007</a></span>). However, as the data collection was based on a free-recall, free-choice survey and semi-structured interviews, the results are susceptible to participant bias. Although achieving a 100% response rate for a survey is not critical to the results (see methodology—data collection section), surveys still have the possibility for errors even with a high response rate. It should also be noted that a single SNA cannot fully address the dynamic nature of complex systems but only provides a stationary viewpoint of the production context, thus creating limitations vis-à-vis the robustness of the results.</p><p class="last">Because the study included a limited number of cases, both in Finland, some restrictions might occur in generalising the results, especially to different cultural and geographical locations. As only one case from each category (centralised/decentralised approach) was examined, each with a limited population of data points, certain limitations may be encountered in generalising the results due to possible specialties of the cases and their implementation strategies. It should also be noted that limitations for conclusively assessing the effect of PP&amp;C approaches on overall production performance and productivity exist, as both projects were ongoing during the data collection period.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0006-S2003"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1527">Avenues for future research</h3><p class="last">First, future research should examine how implementing this study’s suggested improvement actions could advance construction PP&amp;C in practice. These initiatives could also be combined with the development of prevalent PP&amp;C methods, such as the location-based management system (LBMS) or takt production. Such research could also include further analysis of the structure of micro-level decisions to better combine them with formal PP&amp;C processes. Second, in addition to examining project-level studies, future research could examine how production crews’ perspectives could be inspected in a larger perspective, combined with insights into a possible cultural change. This approach would be essential to diminish the epistemological gap between managers and crews in the long term. Third, future research could address how decentralisation and the suggested improvement actions could be facilitated through digital innovation. In particular, an interesting avenue for future research would be to examine how transparency of production status and information flow (which would contribute to better recognising PP&amp;C responsibilities and enabling continuous improvement) could be supported through digitalisation.</p></div></div><div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="scholixLinksDropZone"><!-- Empty dropzone --></div><div id="ack" class="NLM_sec"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1547">Acknowledgments</h3><p class="last">The authors would like to gratefully thank Eemeli Könkkölä, Kiia Haapasalmi, Riikka Joona, and Tuomas Poukka for the assistance in data collection. We would also like to thank the Editor and the anonymous reviewers for their contribution to the peer review of this work, which allowed us to improve the contribution substantially.</p></div><div class=\"captionText\"><span class=\"captionLabel\">Table 2. <\/span> Descriptions of relevant observations and their interpretation.<\/p><\/div><\/caption><thead><tr><th align=\"left\">Domain<\/th><th align=\"center\">Interpretation<\/th><\/tr><\/thead><tbody><tr><td align=\"left\">SNA Network structure analysis<\/td><td align=\"left\"\/><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">Tie characteristics<\/td><td align=\"left\">Ties reflect the quality and amount of communication between actors, divided into strong and weak ties. Strong ties represent a high amount of information exchange between actors, fostering effectiveness and exchange of tacit knowledge (Granovetter <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0027\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>1973<\/a><\/span>). Even though a high amount of strong ties denotes effectiveness within a certain group of individuals, weak ties are also vital to disperse novel information outside of the core group, preventing group thinking and fostering long-term creativity (Hansen <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0029\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>2002<\/a><\/span>).<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">Factions and network density<\/td><td align=\"left\">Factions denote a group of actors who share a relatively high amount of strong ties (Loosemore <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0054\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>1999<\/a><\/span>), forming local, high density concentrations. High density correlates with smooth information flow within the faction, resulting in high team performance, trust and transparency ( Chinoswky <i>et al.<\/i> <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0013\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>2008<\/a><\/span>, Evans &amp; Dion <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0022\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>1991<\/a><\/span>).<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">Power Distribution<\/td><td align=\"left\">Denotes the distribution of authority within the network. Actors who make a large number of decisions and focus on distributing information tend to have more power over others (Chinowsky <i>et al.<\/i> <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0014\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>2010<\/a><\/span>). In centralised networks the power is focussed on a few central actors, while in decentralised networks the power is distributed over factions and individuals.<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">SNA Actor attribute analysis<\/td><td align=\"left\"\/><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">Information consumer\/group member<\/td><td align=\"left\">Information consumers are actors who do not actively contribute to information exchange or giving orders but mostly receive orders and focus on their own tasks. Often characterised by <i>low in-degree centrality<\/i>, indicating that they more likely receive than give or distribute information (Scott <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0086\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>2000<\/a><\/span>). Also tend to possess <i>low closeness centrality<\/i> (indicating how closely an actor is connected to other actors) and\/or <i>low betweenness centrality<\/i> (indicating how often an actor falls in the shortest path of information exchange between two random actors) (Loosemore <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0054\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>1999<\/a><\/span>).<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">Information hub\/liaison<\/td><td align=\"left\">Information hubs \/ liaisons are actors that most actively participate in coordination and problem-solving activities. Often well-connected and transfer information effectively across their peers that would be otherwise disconnected, while often producing creative ideas and sharing them with others (Burt <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0011\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>2004<\/a><\/span>). Liaisons have high influence and power in the network. However, relying too much on them makes the network vulnerable when the actor is absent (Pryke <i>et al.<\/i> <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0076\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>2018<\/a><\/span>). Liaisons are characterised by <i>high closeness centrality and\/or high betweenness centrality<\/i>.<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">Information authority\/star<\/td><td align=\"left\">Information authorities \/ stars are actors with a high amount of influence and power within the network. They actively control information flow and greatly participate in decision-making processes (Hickethier <i>et al.<\/i> <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0031\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>2013<\/a><\/span>). These individuals are critical to the network’s efficiency. Stars often have a <i>high in-degree centrality, high closeness centrality, and high eigenvector centrality<\/i> (indicates how well an individual is connected to other well-connected individuals) (Wasserman &amp; Faust <span class=\"ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr\"><a data-rid=\"CIT0095\" href=\"#\" data-behaviour=\"toggle-ref\" data-ref-type=\"bibr\" data-label=\"reference\"><span class=\"off-screen\">Citation<\/span>1994<\/a><\/span>).<\/td><\/tr><\/tbody><\/table><\/div>","id":"t0002"},{"settings":{"hasCsvFormat":true},"content":"<div id=\"table-content-ut0001\"><table class=\"topbot\"><thead><tr><th align=\"left\">Social network analysis survey form<\/th><\/tr><\/thead><tbody><tr><td align=\"left\">The questionnaire consists of five sections:<br\/>i) general questions (such as the role in the project and organisation)<br\/>ii) questions regarding general communication (information flow network)<br\/>iii) power structures (decision-making network)<br\/>iv) specific work tasks related to the crews inspected in the study (planning and control networks, including crew\/task-specific sub-questions)<br\/>v) open-ended questions regarding decision-making (employed to broaden and support interview data qualitatively)<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">In sections i through iv, subjects were asked to identify project participants with whom they communicated in general, to whom they had given orders\/taken orders from, and with whom they had communicated regarding the planning or control of the specific work task within the last month. The respondents were also asked to rate the frequency of the communication (from 1 to 5; 1 = once or twice per month, 5 = daily) and the mode of communication (email\/project bank\/phone\/face-to-face\/meeting\/other) with each identified participant.<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\"><b>i) General questions<\/b><\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">Which organisation\/company do you work for, and what is your role there?<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">What is your role in the project?<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\"><b>ii) General communication<\/b><\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">With whom have you communicated within the last month concerning the project? Please name all the people and their organisations and roles in the project.<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\"><b>iii) Power structures<\/b><\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">From whom do you receive information that is critical in planning your own work?<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">To whom do you provide information that is critical for others work planning \/ project management?<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\"><b>iv) Specific work tasks related to the studied crews<\/b><\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">With whom have you communicated within the last month concerning the planning or control of the specified work task?<br\/>[The respondents were asked to answer the question individually for every selected work task.]<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\"><b>v) Open-ended questions regarding decision-making<\/b><\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\">How often do you make decisions by yourself regarding your own planning of work?<br\/>Please provide two or three examples of situations where you make decisions by yourself.<\/td><\/tr><\/tbody><\/table><\/div>","id":"ut0001"},{"settings":{"hasCsvFormat":true},"content":"<div id=\"table-content-ut0002\"><table class=\"topbot\"><tbody><tr><td colspan=\"2\"><b>i) General questions and reflection of SNA results<\/b><\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\"\/><td align=\"left\">What is your role in the project, and which tasks does it include?<\/td><\/tr><tr><td align=\"left\"\/><td align=\"left\">How long have you been working on the project? <\/a><a class=\"entryAuthor\" href=\"\/action\/doSearch?Contrib=Peltokorpi%2C+Antti\"><span class=\"NLM_string-name\">Antti Peltokorpi<\/span><a class=\"orchid\" href=\"https:\/\/orcid.org\/0000-0002-7939-6612\"><img src=\"\/templates\/jsp\/_style2\/_tandf\/images\/orcid.svg\" height=\"16px\" width=\"16px\" title=\"ORCID Icon\" alt=\"ORCID Icon\"\/><\/a><\/a><\/div><\/div><div class=\"articleLowerInfo articleInfoSection\"><div class=\"articleLowerInfoSection articleInfoDOI\"><a href=\"https:\/\/doi.org\/10.1080\/01446193.2022.2039399\">https:\/\/doi.org\/10.1080\/01446193.2022.2039399<\/a><\/div><div class=\"articleInfoPublicationDate articleLowerInfoSection border\"><h6>Published online:<\/h6>21 February 2022<\/div><\/div><\/div>","doi":"10.1080\/01446193.2022.2039399"},"figure-index-map":{"F0001":0,"F0002":1,"F0003":2,"F0004":3}};</script><div id="infos-holder"><h2>Additional information</h2><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="Funding">Funding</h3><div class="funding-statement">The research in this paper has been conducted and financially supported under Digitalizing Construction Workflows (DiCtion) research project funded by Business Finland and a consortium of six industrial partners, by Functioning Street Projects (“ToKaHa”) research project funded by the City of Helsinki, and by Building 2030 consortium under projects “Waste in construction design and production” and “Decentralized planning and control in construction” which were funded by a consortium of 21 industrial partners. We are grateful for all provided financial and technical support. </div></div><h2 id="inline_frontnotes">Notes</h2><div class="summation-section"><div id="EN0001"><p class="first last">1 According to the <i>Merriam-Webster Dictionary</i> (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0063" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>n.d</a></span>.) and Mintzberg (<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="CIT0066" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference" data-registered=""><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>1983</a></span>), “decentralization” refers to a process whereby the power of decision-making is dispersed from a central authority to lower levels of hierarchy, such as to teams and/or individuals. In construction production management, decentralization could mean distributing the PP&amp;C authority from general contractor project and site managers to production crews, allowing them certain autonomy in decision-making.</p></div></div><div class="pb-dropzone no-border-top" data-pb-dropzone="contentNavigationDropZoneFull">

href="/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_3_4_101_1&amp;dbid=128&amp;doi=10.1080%2F01446193.2022.2039399&amp;key=000431470100003&amp;getFTLinkType=true&amp;doiForPubOfPage=10.1080%2F01446193.2022.2039399&amp;refDoi=10.1016%2Fj.autcon.2018.03.009&amp;linkType=ISI&amp;linkSource=FULL_TEXT&amp;linkLocation=Reference" target="_blank"><span class="off-screen">(Open in a new window)</span>Web of Science ®</a></span><span class="googleScholar-container"><a class="google-scholar" href="/action/getFTRLinkout?url=http%3A%2F%2Fscholar.google.com%2Fscholar_lookup%3Fhl%3Den%26volume%3D91%26publication_year%3D2018%26pages%3D31-43%26journal%3DAutomation%2Bin%2Bconstruction%26author%3DL.%2BZhang%26author%3DB.%2BAshuri%26title%3DBIM%2Blog%2Bmining%253A%2Bdiscovering%2Bsocial%2Bnetworks&amp;doi=10.1080%2F01446193.2022.2039399&amp;doiOfLink=10.1016%2Fj.autcon.2018.03.009&amp;linkType=gs&amp;linkLocation=Reference&amp;linkSource=FULL_TEXT" target="_blank"><span class="off-screen">(Open in a new window)</span>Google Scholar</a></span></div></span></li></ul></div></div><div id="appendixes"><div class="appendix"><h3 class="section-heading-3">Appendices </h3><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0008"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e4202">Appendix 1: Social network analysis survey form</h2><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="S0009"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e4204">Introduction</h3><p>The survey consists of five sections:</p><ol class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-roman-lower"><li><p class="first last">General information (such as the role in the project and organization)</p></li><li><p class="first last">General communication (information-flow network)</p></li><li><p class="first last">Power structures (decision-making network)</p></li><li><p class="first last">Specific work tasks related to the crews inspected in the study (planning and control networks, including crew/task-specific sub-questions)</p></li><li><p class="first last">Open-ended questions about decision-making (employed to broaden and support interview data qualitatively)</p></li></ol><p></p><p class="last">In sections i through iv, subjects were asked to identify project participants with whom they communicated, to whom they had given/taken orders, and with whom they had communicated about the planning or control of the specific work task within the last month. The respondents were also asked to rate the frequency of the communication (from 1 to 5; 1 = once or twice per month, 5 = daily) and the mode of communication (email/project bank/phone/face-to-face/in a meeting) with each participant.</p><ol class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-roman-lower"><li><p class="first"><b>General questions</b></p><p>Which organisation do you work for, and what is your role in the organisation?</p><p class="last">What is your role in the project?</p></li><li><p class="first"><b>General communication</b></p><p class="last">With whom have you communicated within the last month concerning the project? Please name all the people and their organisations and roles in the project.</p></li><li><p class="first"><b>Power structures</b></p><p>From whom do you receive information that is critical in planning your own work?</p><p class="last">To whom do you provide information that is critical for others’ work planning/project management?</p></li><li><p class="first"><b>Specific work tasks related to the studied crews</b></p><p>With whom have you communicated within the last month concerning the planning or control of the specified work task?</p><p class="last">[The respondents were asked to answer the question individually for every selected work task.]</p></li><li><p class="first"><b>Open-ended questions about decision-making</b></p><p>How often do you make decisions by yourself regarding your own planning of work?</p><p class="last">Please provide two or three examples of situations where you make decisions by yourself.</p></li></ol><p></p></div></div><h3 class="section-heading-3">Appendix 2: </h3><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="S0010"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e4248">Interview form</h2><p><b>i) General questions and reflection of SNA results </b></p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">What is your role in the project, and which tasks does it include?</p></li><li><p class="first last">How long have you been working on the project?</p></li><li><p class="first last">From your point of view, how has the project progressed?</p></li><li><p class="first last">[Brief reflection of the SNA results]</p></li></ul><p></p><p><b>ii) General communication </b></p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">With whom do you mostly communicate about this project?</p></li><li><p class="first last">What kind of information do you provide and receive?</p></li><li><p class="first last">What kind of information do you need to excel in your work?</p></li><li><p class="first last">What kind of information do others need from you?</p></li><li><p class="first last">What kind of challenges occur in your daily work, and how do you solve them?</p></li></ul><p></p><p><b>iii a) Production planning and control (project/site management) </b></p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">What tasks/works are currently in progress?</p></li><li><p class="first last">How are the plans made? What is the process, and who is involved?</p></li><li><p class="first last">How are the tasks controlled? At what intervals, and who makes the decisions?</p></li><li><p class="first last">How do you participate in decision-making?</p></li><li><p class="first last">Are there instances when you are able to perform autonomously? Please provide examples.</p></li><li><p class="first last">Do you feel you have the resources to perform in situations that require rapid decision-making?</p></li><li><p class="first last">Do you feel you are able to get the information you need to make decisions?</p></li></ul><p></p><p><b>iii b) Production planning and control (crew leaders/workers) </b></p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">What tasks/steps does your work involve? In what order do you perform your work?</p></li><li><p class="first last">How are the plans made? What is the process, and who is involved?</p></li><li><p class="first last">How are the tasks controlled? At what intervals, and who makes the decisions?</p></li><li><p class="first last">Are you able to participate in decision-making?</p></li><li><p class="first last">Are there instances when you are able to perform your work autonomously? Please provide examples.</p></li><li><p class="first last">Do you feel you have the resources to perform in situations that require rapid decision-making?</p></li><li><p class="first last">Do you feel you are able to get the information you need to make decisions?</p></li></ul><p></p><p><b>iv) Possible improvement actions </b></p><ul class="NLM_list NLM_list-list_type-bullet"><li><p class="first last">How would you improve the way in which planning and control is carried out?</p></li><li><p class="first last">How would you improve the communication and organizational structures?</p></li><li><p class="first last">What are your insights on decentralized/centralized planning and control?</p></li></ul><p></p><div class="tableView"><div class="tableCaption"><p class="fulltext" align="left">Table</p></div><div class="tableDownloadOption" id="ut0001-table-wrapper"><a class="downloadButton btn btn-sm" role="button" href="/action/downloadTable?id=ut0001&amp;doi=10.1080%2F01446193.2022.2039399&amp;downloadType=CSV">Download CSV</a><a data-id="ut0001" class="downloadButton btn btn-sm displaySizeTable" href="#" role="button" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Display Table</a></div></div><div class="tableView"><div class="tableCaption"><p class="fulltext" align="left">Table</p></div><div class="tableDownloadOption" id="ut0002-table-wrapper"><a class="downloadButton btn btn-sm" role="button" href="/action/downloadTable?id=ut0002&amp;doi=10.1080%2F01446193.2022.2039399&amp;downloadType=CSV">Download CSV</a><a data-id="ut0002" class="downloadButton btn btn-sm displaySizeTable" href="#" role="button" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="table" data-registered="">Display Table</a></div></div></div></div></div>
                            
                            
                        
                    
                    

















                </article>
            </div>
